Quasi-neutral limit of Nernst-Planck-Navier-Stokes system
Ping Zhang, Yibin Zhang

Abstract
. In this paper, we investigate the quasi-neutral limit of Nernst-Planck-Navier-Stokes system in a smooth bounded domain Ω \Omega of ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d} for d = 2 , 3 , d=2,3, with “electroneutral boundary conditions” and well-prepared data. We first prove by using modulated energy estimate that the solution sequence converges to the limit system in the norm of L ∞ ​ ( ( 0 , T ) , L 2 ​ ( Ω ) ) L^{\infty}((0,T);L^{2}(\Omega)) for some positive time T . T. In order to justify the limit in a stronger norm, we need to construct both the initial layers and weak boundary layers in the approximate solutions.



Keywords:
Nernst-Planck-Navier-Stokes system;  quasi-neutral limit;  modulated energy estimate.

AMS Subject Classification (2000):

82C21; 82D15; 35Q92

1.
Introduction

The Nernst-Planck-Navier-Stokes system, in short NPNS system, describes the time evolution of ionic concentration in solvents,
which are transported by viscous incompressible fluid and diffuse under an electric potential and their own concentration gradients.
The main purpose of this paper is to study the quasi-electroneutral limit (
\varepsilon\rightarrow
0) of the following binary NPNS system on a smooth bounded domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
for
d=2,3,

(1.1)

\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\partial_{t}c_{1}+u\cdot\nabla c_{1}=D_{1}\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits(\nabla c_{1}+z_{1}c_{1}\nabla\Phi),\\
\partial_{t}c_{2}+u\cdot\nabla c_{2}=D_{2}\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits(\nabla c_{2}+z_{2}c_{2}\nabla\Phi),\\
-\varepsilon^{2}\Delta\Phi=\rho=z_{1}c_{1}+z_{2}c_{2},\\
\partial_{t}u+u\cdot\nabla u-\nu\Delta u=-\nabla p-K\rho\nabla\Phi,\\
\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits u=0,\\
c_{1}|_{t=0}=c_{1}(0),\quad c_{2}|_{t=0}=c_{2}(0),\quad u|_{t=0}=u_{0}.\end{array}\right.

The function
c_{i}=c_{i}(x,t)
represents the concentration of the
i
-th species (
i=1,2
),
and
\Phi
is the electrical potential generated by the charge density
\rho.

The divergence-free vector field
u
is the velocity of the fluid, and
p
is a scalar pressure function.

z_{1}>0>z_{2}
,
D_{i}>0
are constants (which may differ from each other), and designate the valency and diffusivity of the
i
-th species, respectively. In what follows, we denote
D^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\min\{D_{1},D_{2}\}
, and without loss of generality, we assume that
D_{1}\geq D_{2}
.
The positive constant
\varepsilon
is a rescaled dielectric permittivity of the solvent and is proportional to the Debye length. The kinematic viscosity of the fluid
\nu>0
, and
K>0
is a constant related to the Boltzmann’s constant and the absolute temperature (for simplicity,
here we take
K=1
). One may check
[
12
]
for an introduction of the
basic physical and mathematical issues about the system (
1.1
).

We consider the electroneutrality (or the vanishing of electronic charge) of NPNS system with “well-prepared” initial data, i.e.,
\rho(0)=0
. In addition, we consider the following “electroneutral boundary conditions” for
c_{i}

(1.2)

(\mathbf{EN})\quad c_{1}|_{\partial\Omega}=\gamma_{1}(x)>0,\quad c_{2}|_{\partial\Omega}=\gamma_{2}(x)>0,\quad z_{1}\gamma_{1}+z_{2}\gamma_{2}=0.

We also implement the system (
1.1
) with Dirichlet boundary conditions for
u
and
\Phi:

u|_{\partial\Omega}=0\quad\hbox{and}\quad\Phi|_{\partial\Omega}=W(x).

Since here we consider the quasineutral limit of the system (
1.1
) in finite time, the boundary data
\gamma_{i}(x)
and
W(x)

are allowed to depend on time. For simplicity, we just take the time independent data.

We denote by
\Gamma_{i}
and
\Phi_{W}
to be determined respectively by

(1.3)

\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\Delta\Gamma_{i}=0,\quad x\in\Omega,\\
\Gamma_{i}|_{\partial\Omega}=\gamma_{i}(x),\end{array}\right.\quad\hbox{and}\quad\ \left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\Delta\Phi_{W}=0,\quad x\in\Omega,\\
\Phi_{W}|_{\partial\Omega}=W(x).\end{array}\right.

Then due to the uniqueness of Poisson equation and (
1.2
), we have
z_{1}\Gamma_{1}+z_{2}\Gamma_{2}=0
.

In the case
d=2
, the global well-posedness of binary NPNS system has been established in
[
5
]
. In the case
d=3
,
the problem of global well-posedness of binary NPNS system has only partial results, especially due to the part of Navier-Stokes equations.
In fact, even the global existence of pure 3D Nernst-Planck system or the system coupled to Stokes flow is in general open. One may check
[
8
,
9
,
10
,
13
]

for the global existence of weak solutions of the 3-D system (
1.1
).
Thus, we can only expect that the lifespan to the strong solution of 3-D NPNS system has a positive lower bound, which is independent of the Debye length
\varepsilon
, and which will be established in Section
2
.

Constantin and Ignatova etal
[
4
,
5
]
first proved the global existence and stability
of solutions to 2-D NPNS system under the blocking boundary conditions or uniform selective boundary conditions (we also mention that the same result was obtained in
[
1
]
with blocking boundary conditions for the ions and a Robin boundary condition for the electric potential). They further proved in
[
6
]
that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\lim_{t\to\infty}\sup_{x\in K}|\rho(t,x)|=0,

for any fixed initial conditions and any compact subset
K
of
\Omega.
They also established the same result in 3D with the same boundary conditions and small perturbations of steady states data. In
[
14
]
, the authors investigated the initial layer for the 3-D system (
1.1
) in
\mathbb{T}^{3}
in the quasi-neutral regime.

Formally, by setting
\varepsilon=0
in the Poisson equation of (
1.1
), one has
\rho=0
and the Nernst-Planck equations and Navier-Stokes equations will be decoupled. To make this scenario mathematically rigorous, we introduce
\psi^{\varepsilon}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\Phi^{\varepsilon}-\Phi_{W}
, and rewrite the NPNS system as

(1.4a)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}c^{\varepsilon}_{1}+u^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla c^{\varepsilon}_{1}=D_{1}\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits(\nabla c^{\varepsilon}_{1}+z_{1}c^{\varepsilon}_{1}\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}+z_{1}c^{\varepsilon}_{1}\nabla\Phi_{W}),

(1.4b)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}c^{\varepsilon}_{2}+u^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla c^{\varepsilon}_{2}=D_{1}\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits(\nabla c^{\varepsilon}_{2}+z_{2}c^{\varepsilon}_{2}\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}+z_{2}c^{\varepsilon}_{2}\nabla\Phi_{W}),

(1.4c)

\displaystyle-\varepsilon^{2}\Delta\psi^{\varepsilon}=\rho^{\varepsilon}=z_{1}c^{\varepsilon}_{1}+z_{2}c^{\varepsilon}_{2},

(1.4d)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u^{\varepsilon}+u^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla u^{\varepsilon}-\nu\Delta u^{\varepsilon}=-\nabla p^{\varepsilon}-\rho^{\varepsilon}\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}-\rho^{\varepsilon}\nabla\Phi_{W}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad=-\nabla p^{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon^{2}\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits(\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}\otimes\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon})-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\nabla\lvert\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}\rvert^{2}-\rho^{\varepsilon}\nabla\Phi_{W},

(1.4e)

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits u^{\varepsilon}=0.

Here we have used the relation

\Delta\psi^{\varepsilon}\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}=\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits(\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}\otimes\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon})-\frac{1}{2}\nabla\lvert\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}\rvert^{2}.

And we implement the following boundary conditions for the system (
1.4
):

(1.5)

c^{\varepsilon}_{1}|_{\partial\Omega}=\gamma_{i}(x),\quad u^{\varepsilon}|_{\partial\Omega}=0,\quad\psi^{\varepsilon}|_{\partial\Omega}=0,\quad z_{1}\gamma_{1}+z_{2}\gamma_{2}=0.

By using modulated energy estimate, we shall prove in Section
3
that as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0,

the solutions of the system (
1.4
) with “well-prepared” initial data converge to the following decoupled system:

(1.6a)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}c_{1}+u\cdot\nabla c_{1}=D_{1}\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits(\nabla c_{1}+z_{1}c_{1}\nabla\psi+z_{1}c_{1}\nabla\Phi_{W}),

(1.6b)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}c_{2}+u\cdot\nabla c_{2}=D_{2}\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits(\nabla c_{2}+z_{2}c_{2}\nabla\psi+z_{2}c_{2}\nabla\Phi_{W}),

(1.6c)

\displaystyle\rho=z_{1}c_{1}+z_{2}c_{2}=0,

(1.6d)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u+u\cdot\nabla u-\nu\Delta u=-\nabla p,

(1.6e)

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits u=0,

(1.6f)

\displaystyle c_{i}|_{\partial\Omega}=\gamma_{i}(x),\quad\psi|_{\partial\Omega}=0,\quad u|_{\partial\Omega}=0,\quad z_{1}\gamma_{1}+z_{2}\gamma_{2}=0.

By combining equations (
1.6a
), (
1.6b
), (
1.6c
) and boundary conditions(
1.6f
),
\psi
is the unique solution of the following non-degenerate second order elliptic equation

(1.7)

\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits\big((z_{1}D_{1}\nabla c_{1}+z_{2}D_{2}\nabla c_{2})+(z_{1}^{2}D_{1}c_{1}+z_{2}^{2}D_{2}c_{2})(\nabla\psi+\nabla\Phi_{W})\big)=0,\\
\psi|_{\partial\Omega}=0.\end{array}\right.

In view of (
1.6c
), we can equivalently rewrite (
1.7
) as

(1.8)

\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits\big((D_{1}-D_{2})\nabla c_{1}+(z_{1}D_{1}-z_{2}D_{2})c_{1}(\nabla\psi+\nabla\Phi_{W})\big)=0,\\
\psi|_{\partial\Omega}=0.\end{array}\right.

Remark 1.1
.

In Section
2
, we shall prove by
maximum principle that
z_{1}^{2}D_{1}c_{1}+z_{2}^{2}D_{2}c_{2}
has a positive lower bound as long as
z_{1}^{2}D_{1}c_{1}(0)+z_{2}^{2}D_{2}c_{2}(0)
is strictly positive.
So that the equation (
1.7
) is strictly elliptic.

Furthermore, by plugging equations (
1.6c
) and (
1.7
) into equations (
1.6a
) and (
1.6b
),
we obtain the following equation for
c_{i}
(
i=1,2
):

(1.9)

\partial_{t}c_{i}+u\cdot\nabla c_{i}=\frac{(z_{1}-z_{2})D_{1}D_{2}}{z_{1}D_{1}-z_{2}D_{2}}\Delta c_{i}.

In what follows, we always denote

(1.10a)

\displaystyle\lambda^{\varepsilon}_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\min\bigl\{\inf_{\partial\Omega}\gamma_{i},\inf_{\Omega}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}(0)\bigr\},\qquad\Lambda^{\varepsilon}_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\max\bigl\{\sup_{\partial\Omega}\gamma_{i},\sup_{\Omega}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}(0)\bigr\},

(1.10b)

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\min\bigl\{\inf_{\partial\Omega}\gamma_{i},\inf_{\Omega}c_{i}(0)\bigr\},\qquad\Lambda_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\max\bigl\{\sup_{\partial\Omega}\gamma_{i},\sup_{\Omega}c_{i}(0)\bigr\},

and we denote by
A
to be the Stokes operator with domain
D(A)\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}H^{2}(\Omega)\cap H^{1}_{0,\sigma}(\Omega)
, and denote by
V
to be the space
D(A^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
We always assume that there exist
0<\lambda\leq\Lambda<\infty
such that

(1.11)

0<\lambda\leq\min\{\lambda^{\varepsilon}_{i},\lambda_{i}\}\leq\max\{\Lambda^{\varepsilon}_{i},\Lambda_{i}\}\leq\Lambda<\infty.

Our first main result of this paper states as follows:

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
d=2
or
3
, and the initial data be “well-prepared” i.e.
\rho^{\varepsilon}(0)=0.
We assume
(c^{\varepsilon}_{i}(0),u^{\varepsilon}(0),c_{i}(0),u(0))\in H^{5}
.
If there exists a positive constant
C>0
so that

(1.12a)

\displaystyle\|c^{\varepsilon}_{i}(0)-c_{i}(0)\|_{L^{2}}+\|u^{\varepsilon}(0)-u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\varepsilon,

(1.12b)

\displaystyle\|u^{\varepsilon}(0)\|_{V}\leq C,

then there exist positive constants
M,T>0,
which depend only on initial data,
\nu,\lambda,\Lambda,W(x)
,
\gamma_{i}(x),z_{i}
and
D_{i}
for
i=1,2,
so that

(1.13a)

\displaystyle\|c^{\varepsilon}_{i}-c_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{2})}+\|u^{\varepsilon}-u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{2})}+\varepsilon\|\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{2})}\leq M\varepsilon,

(1.13b)

\displaystyle\|\nabla c^{\varepsilon}_{i}-\nabla c_{i}\|_{L^{2}_{T}(L^{2})}+\|\nabla u^{\varepsilon}-\nabla u\|_{L^{2}_{T}(L^{2})}+\|\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}-\nabla\psi\|_{L^{2}_{T}(L^{2})}\leq M\varepsilon,

(1.13c)

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{-1}\|\rho^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}_{T}(L^{2})}=\varepsilon\|\Delta\psi^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}_{T}(L^{2})}\leq M\varepsilon.

Remark 1.2
.

(1)

Due to the “well-prepared” assumption,
\psi^{\varepsilon}(0)
equals to zero and doesn’t appear in our assumption (
1.12a
).

(2)

(
1.12b
) is not required in the case d=2.

(3)

The main idea used to prove Theorem
1.1
is to use modulated energy estimate,
which was first introduced by Brenier
[
2
]
to study the quasi-neutral limit of
Vlasov-Poisson system to the incompressible Euler equations, which motivates the first author of this
paper to investigate the semi-classical limit of Schrödinger-Poisson equations through Wigner transform (
[
17
]
).
The “modulated energy functional” in
[
17
]
was simplified by the authors in
[
11
]
in order to deal with
the semi-classical limit of cubic Schödinger equation in the exterior domain.

In order to consider the convergence in (
1.13
) in stronger norm, we need to investigate both the initial layers
and boundary layers. For simplicity, we just take
\Omega=\mathbb{T}^{d-1}\times(0,1)
for
d=2,3.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
d=2
or
3
, and the initial data be “well-prepared” i.e.
\rho^{\varepsilon}(0)=0.
We assume
(c^{\varepsilon}_{i}(0),u^{\varepsilon}(0),c_{i}(0),u(0))\in H^{5}
.
If there exists a positive constant
C>0
so that

(1.14a)

\displaystyle\|c^{\varepsilon}_{i}(0)-c_{i}(0)\|_{L^{2}}+\|u^{\varepsilon}(0)-u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\varepsilon^{3},

(1.14b)

\displaystyle\|\nabla c^{\varepsilon}_{i}(0)-\nabla c_{i}(0)\|_{L^{2}}+\|u^{\varepsilon}(0)-u_{0}\|_{V}\leq C\varepsilon^{\frac{3}{2}},

(1.14c)

\displaystyle\|\Delta c^{\varepsilon}_{i}(0)-\Delta c_{i}(0)\|_{L^{2}}+\|Au^{\varepsilon}(0)-Au_{0}\|_{L^{2}}\leq\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}},

(1.14d)

\displaystyle\psi(0)=0,

then there exist positive constants
\varepsilon_{0},M,T>0,
which depend only on initial data,
\nu,\lambda,\Lambda,

W(x),\gamma_{i}(x),z_{i}
and
D_{i}
for
i=1,2,
so that for
\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
,

\|c^{\varepsilon}_{i}-c_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(H^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d-1}\times(0,1)))}+\|\rho^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(H^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d-1}\times(0,1)))}\leq M\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Remark 1.3
.

In fact, we shall present more precise expansions for
c^{\varepsilon}_{i}
and
\rho^{\varepsilon}
in Theorem
5.1
below.
One may check (
5.1
) and (
5.14
) for details.

We end this section with the structure of this paper.

In Section
2
, we shall prove that the
\varepsilon
-dependent NPNS system has a strong solution on some
\varepsilon
-independent time interval.

In Section
3
, we present the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

In Section
4
, we investigate both the initial and boundary layers of the
\varepsilon
-dependent NPNS system.

Finally in Section
5
, we present the proof of Theorem
1.2
. In fact, we shall present
more detailed approximation, see Theorems
5.1
and
5.2
.

2.
Preliminaries and technical lemmas

In this section, we present some relevant results and some basic lemmas. We first define the electrochemical potentials as follows:

(2.1)

\mu^{\varepsilon}_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\log c^{\varepsilon}_{i}+z_{i}(\psi^{\varepsilon}+\Phi_{W}),\quad\mu^{*}_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\log\Gamma_{i}+z_{i}\Phi_{W}\quad\hbox{and}\quad\mu_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\log c_{i}+z_{i}(\psi+\Phi_{W}).

Then we may rewrite the mass conservation equations (
1.4a
-
1.4b
) and (
1.6a
-
1.6b
) of
c^{\varepsilon}_{i}
and
c_{i}
as

(2.2)

\partial_{t}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}=\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits(D_{i}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}\nabla\mu^{\varepsilon}_{i}-u^{\varepsilon}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}),

and

(2.3)

\partial_{t}c_{i}=\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits(D_{i}c_{i}\nabla\mu_{i}-uc_{i}).

In the case of
z_{1}=-z_{2},
the positive lower bound of
c^{\varepsilon}_{i}
for the system (
1.1
)
with Dirichlet boundary condition can be obtained by using maximum principle (see
[
6
,
7
]
).
Along the same lines to
[
6
]
, we shall prove similar result for the system (
1.1
) with
“electro-neutral boundary conditions” (
1.2
) and “well-prepared” initial data. However,
z_{1}
and
-z_{2}
are relaxed to arbitrary positive constants.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
(c^{\varepsilon}_{1},c^{\varepsilon}_{2},\psi^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon})
be smooth enough solution of the system (
1.4a
-
1.4e
) with boundary conditions (
1.5
) on
[0,T].

We assume that the initial data is “well-prepared” (i.e.
\rho^{\varepsilon}(0)=0
). Then
c^{\varepsilon}_{i}(i=1,2)
verifies

0<\lambda^{\varepsilon}_{i}\leq c^{\varepsilon}_{i}(t,x)\leq\Lambda^{\varepsilon}_{i}

for
\lambda^{\varepsilon}_{i}
and
\Lambda^{\varepsilon}_{i}
being defined by (
1.10a
).

Proof.

Notice that
z_{1}>0
and
z_{2}<0,
we deduce from (
1.2
) and
\rho^{\varepsilon}(0)=0
that

z_{1}\lambda_{1}^{\varepsilon}+z_{2}\lambda_{2}^{\varepsilon}=0\quad\hbox{and}\quad z_{1}\Lambda_{1}^{\varepsilon}+z_{2}\Lambda_{2}^{\varepsilon}=0.

In what follows, we just prove
c_{1}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)\leq\Lambda_{1}^{\varepsilon}
.
Otherwise, we denote
m_{i}^{\varepsilon}(t)\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\sup_{x\in\Omega}c_{i}^{\varepsilon}(x,t)
and fix a positive constant
\kappa>\Lambda_{1}^{\varepsilon},
then
there exists some
t\in(0,T)
so that

m_{1}^{\varepsilon}(t)\geq\kappa
. We denote
t_{0}>0
to be the first time when
m_{1}^{\varepsilon}(t_{0})=\kappa
is attained.
Since
\kappa>\Lambda_{1}^{\varepsilon}\geq\sup_{\partial\Omega}\gamma_{1}^{\varepsilon}
, there exists an interior point
x_{0}\in\Omega
so that

c_{1}^{\varepsilon}(x_{0},t_{0})=\kappa
. Hence using the the equation (
1.4a
), (
1.4c
) and maximality at point
(x_{0},t_{0})
, we find

0\leq\partial_{t}c_{1}^{\varepsilon}(x_{0},t_{0})\leq-\frac{z_{1}D_{1}}{\varepsilon^{2}}c_{1}^{\varepsilon}(x_{0},t_{0})\big(z_{1}c_{1}^{\varepsilon}(x_{0},t_{0})+z_{2}c_{2}^{\varepsilon}(x_{0},t_{0})\big),

where we used the facts that the gradient of
c_{1}^{\varepsilon}
vanishes at
(x_{0},t_{0}),

and the Laplacian is non-positive at an interior maximal point. As a result, it comes out

z_{1}c_{1}^{\varepsilon}(x_{0},t_{0})+z_{2}c_{2}^{\varepsilon}(x_{0},t_{0})\leq 0,

which implies

(2.4)

c_{2}^{\varepsilon}(x_{0},t_{0})\geq-\frac{z_{1}}{z_{2}}c_{1}^{\varepsilon}(x_{0},t_{0})\geq-\frac{z_{1}}{z_{2}}\kappa>-\frac{z_{1}}{z_{2}}\Lambda_{1}^{\varepsilon}=\Lambda_{2}^{\varepsilon}.

So that
we can choose the first time
0<t_{1}\leq t_{0}
when
m_{2}^{\varepsilon}(t_{1})=-\frac{z_{1}}{z_{2}}\kappa.
We claim that
t_{1}=t_{0}
.
Otherwise by repeating the proof of (
2.4
), we can choose the first time
0<t_{2}\leq t_{1}<t_{0}
when
m_{1}^{\varepsilon}(t_{2})=\kappa,
which contradicts with the definition of
t_{0}
.

Since
m_{i}^{\varepsilon}(t_{0})>\Lambda_{i}^{\varepsilon}
(
i=1,2
), by continuity, there exists
\delta>0
such that for all
s\in[t_{0}-\delta,t_{0}]
, there exist interior points
x_{i}(s)\in\Omega
so that
c_{i}^{\varepsilon}(x_{i}(s),s)=m_{i}^{\varepsilon}(s)>\Lambda_{i}^{\varepsilon}
. Then for any
t_{0}-\delta<r<s\leq t_{0}
, we have

(2.5)

\begin{split}\limsup_{r\rightarrow s^{-}}\frac{m_{1}^{\varepsilon}(s)-m_{1}^{\varepsilon}(r)}{s-r}\leq\limsup_{r\rightarrow s^{-}}\frac{c_{1}^{\varepsilon}(x_{1}(s),s)-c_{1}^{\varepsilon}(x_{1}(s),r)}{s-r}=\partial_{t}c_{1}^{\varepsilon}(x_{1}(s),s)\\
\leq-\frac{z_{1}D_{1}}{\varepsilon^{2}}m_{1}^{\varepsilon}(s)(z_{1}m_{1}^{\varepsilon}(s)+z_{2}m_{2}^{\varepsilon}(s))\leq-\frac{z_{1}^{2}D_{1}}{2\varepsilon^{2}}(m^{\varepsilon}_{1})^{2}(s)+\frac{z_{2}^{2}D_{1}}{2\varepsilon^{2}}(m^{\varepsilon}_{2})^{2}(s).\end{split}

Along the same line, we obtain

(2.6)

\limsup_{r\rightarrow s^{-}}\frac{m_{2}^{\varepsilon}(s)-m^{\varepsilon}_{2}(r)}{s-r}\leq\frac{z_{1}^{2}D_{2}}{2\varepsilon^{2}}(m^{\varepsilon}_{1})^{2}(s)-\frac{z_{2}^{2}D_{2}}{2\varepsilon^{2}}(m^{\varepsilon}_{2})^{2}(s).

By multiplying (
2.5
) by
D_{2}
and (
2.6
) by
D_{1},
and then summing up the resulting inequalities, we achieve

\limsup_{r\rightarrow s^{-}}\Bigl(D_{2}\frac{m_{1}^{\varepsilon}(s)-m_{1}^{\varepsilon}(r)}{s-r}+D_{1}\frac{m_{2}^{\varepsilon}(s)-m_{2}^{\varepsilon}(r)}{s-r}\Bigr)\leq 0.

Then we deduce from Lemma
2.2
below that
D_{2}m_{1}^{\varepsilon}(s)+D_{1}{m_{2}^{\varepsilon}(s)}
is a non-increasing function on
[t_{0}-\delta,t_{0}].

As a consequence, we obtain

D_{2}m_{1}^{\varepsilon}(t_{0}-\delta)+D_{1}m_{2}^{\varepsilon}(t_{0}-\delta)\geq D_{2}m_{1}^{\varepsilon}(t_{0})+D_{1}m_{2}^{\varepsilon}(t_{0})=D_{2}\kappa-\frac{z_{1}}{z_{2}}D_{1}\kappa.

Hence we have
m_{1}^{\varepsilon}(t_{0}-\delta)\geq\kappa
, or
m_{2}^{\varepsilon}(t_{0}-\delta)\geq-\frac{z_{1}}{z_{2}}\kappa,
either of which contradicts with our choice of
t_{0}
. This completes the proof of Lemma
2.1
.
∎

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
f:(a,b]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
satisfies

(2.7)

\limsup_{r\rightarrow s^{-}}\frac{f(s)-f(r)}{s-r}\leq 0\quad\mbox{for any}\ \ s\in(a,b].

Then f is non-increasing on
(a,b]
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, it suffices to fix some
c\in(a,b)
and prove
f(c)\geq f(b)
. In view of
(
2.7
), for any
\epsilon>0
and
s\in(a,b]
, there exists
\delta_{s}>0
such that for any
r\in(s-\delta_{s},s)
, we have
\frac{f(s)-f(r)}{s-r}\leq\epsilon
. Thus
\{(s-\delta_{s},s)|s\in(a,b)\}
constitutes an open covering of closed interval
[c,b-\delta_{b}]
, and by finite covering theorem, there exists a set of finite open intervals
\{(s_{k}-\delta_{k},s_{k})\}_{k=1}^{n}
such that

s_{1}-\delta_{1}<c<s_{2}-\delta_{2}<s_{1}<s_{3}-\delta_{3}<s_{2}<\dots s_{n}-\delta_{n}<s_{n-1}<b-\delta_{b}<s_{n}<b,

and

\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}f(s_{1})-f(c)\leq\epsilon(s_{1}-c),&k=1,\\
f(s_{k})-f(s_{k-1})\leq\epsilon(s_{k}-s_{k-1}),&2\leq k\leq n,\\
f(b)-f(s_{n})\leq\epsilon(b-s_{n}).&\end{array}\right.

By summing up the above inequalities for
k
from
1
to
n,
we achieve
f(b)-f(c)\leq\epsilon(b-c)
, and then letting
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
leads to
f(b)\leq f(c).
This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Remark 2.1
.

Since
c_{i}
satisfies a transport-diffusion equation (
1.9
), it’s easy to deduce the same result for
c_{i}
, i.e.
\lambda_{i}\leq c_{i}\leq\Lambda_{i}
with
\lambda_{i}
and
\Lambda_{i}
being defined by (
1.10b
).

In the subsequent lemma, we shall use the dissipative structure of the system (
1.4
) to derive the estimate for the energy functional
E^{\varepsilon}(t).

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
(c^{\varepsilon}_{1},c^{\varepsilon}_{2},u^{\varepsilon})
be a smooth enough solution of the system (
1.4
) supplemented with the boundary conditions (
1.5
) on interval
[0,T]
, we define energy functional
E^{\varepsilon}(t)
via

(2.8)

E^{\varepsilon}(t)\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\sum^{2}_{i=1}\int_{\Omega}\Gamma_{i}\varphi\bigl(\frac{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}{\Gamma_{i}}\bigr)\,dx+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\|\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}(t)\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\|u^{\varepsilon}(t)\|^{2}_{L^{2}},

where
\varphi(s)=s\log s-s+1\geq 0
.
Then there hold

(1)

(2.9)

\begin{split}&\frac{d}{dt}E^{\varepsilon}(t)+\nu\|\nabla u^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\sum^{2}_{i=1}D_{i}\int_{\Omega}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}|\nabla\mu^{\varepsilon}_{i}|^{2}\,dx\\
&=-\sum^{2}_{i=1}\int_{\Omega}u^{\varepsilon}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}\nabla\log\Gamma_{i}\,dx-\int_{\Omega}u^{\varepsilon}\rho^{\varepsilon}\nabla\Phi_{W}\,dx+\sum^{2}_{i=1}D_{i}\int_{\Omega}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}\nabla\mu^{\varepsilon}_{i}\nabla\mu^{*}_{i}\,dx.\end{split}

(2)

There exists a positive constant
M
depending only on
\Lambda,W(x),\gamma_{i}(x),z_{i}
and
D_{i}
for
i=1,2
, such that for any
t\in[0,T],

(2.10)

E^{\varepsilon}(t)+\frac{\nu}{2}\|\nabla u^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{t}(L^{2})}+\frac{1}{2}\sum^{2}_{i=1}D_{i}\int^{t}_{0}\int_{\Omega}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}|\nabla\mu^{\varepsilon}_{i}|^{2}dxdt\leq(E^{\varepsilon}(0)+Mt)e^{t}.

(3)

There exists a positive constant
M
depending only on
\lambda,\Lambda,z_{i}
and
D_{i}
for
i=1,2
, such that

(2.11)

\|\nabla c^{\varepsilon}_{i}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}+\nabla\Phi_{W}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\|\frac{\rho^{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\leq M\sum^{2}_{i=1}D_{i}\int_{\Omega}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}|\nabla\mu^{\varepsilon}_{i}|^{2}\,dx.

Proof.

Notice that
\frac{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}{\Gamma_{i}}|_{\partial\Omega}=1,
by multiplying (
2.2
) by
\log\frac{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}{\Gamma_{i}}
and
integrating over
\Omega
and then summing up the resulting inequalities for
i=1,2,
we find

\displaystyle\sum^{2}_{i=1}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\Omega}\Gamma_{i}\varphi(\frac{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}{\Gamma_{i}})\,dx=\sum^{2}_{i=1}\int_{\Omega}\partial_{t}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}\log\frac{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}{\Gamma_{i}}\,\,dx

\displaystyle=-\sum^{2}_{i=1}\int_{\Omega}(D_{i}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}\nabla\mu^{\varepsilon}_{i}-u^{\varepsilon}c^{\varepsilon}_{i})(\nabla\log c^{\varepsilon}_{i}-\nabla\log\Gamma_{i})\,dx

\displaystyle=-\sum^{2}_{i=1}D_{i}\int_{\Omega}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}\nabla\mu^{\varepsilon}_{i}\nabla\log c^{\varepsilon}_{i}\,dx+\sum^{2}_{i=1}D_{i}\int_{\Omega}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}\nabla\mu^{\varepsilon}_{i}\nabla\log\Gamma_{i}\,dx-\sum^{2}_{i=1}\int_{\Omega}u^{\varepsilon}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}\nabla\log\Gamma_{i}\,dx,

where we used
\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits u^{\varepsilon}=0
and
u^{\varepsilon}|_{\partial\Omega}=0
in the third equality.

While due to
\psi^{\varepsilon}|_{\partial\Omega}=0,
we get, by using integration by parts and the equations (
1.4c
), (
2.2
), that

\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\frac{d}{dt}

\displaystyle\|\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}(t)\|^{2}_{L^{2}}=\varepsilon^{2}\int_{\Omega}\partial_{t}\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}\,dx=\int_{\Omega}\partial_{t}\rho^{\varepsilon}\psi^{\varepsilon}\,dx

\displaystyle=\sum^{2}_{i=1}\int_{\Omega}\partial_{t}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}(z_{i}\psi^{\varepsilon})\,dx=-\sum^{2}_{i=1}\int_{\Omega}(D_{i}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}\nabla\mu^{\varepsilon}_{i}-u^{\varepsilon}c^{\varepsilon}_{i})(z_{i}\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon})\,dx

\displaystyle=-\sum^{2}_{i=1}z_{i}D_{i}\int_{\Omega}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}\nabla\mu^{\varepsilon}_{i}\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}\,dx+\int_{\Omega}u^{\varepsilon}\rho^{\varepsilon}\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}\,dx.

Whereas by taking
L^{2}
inner product of the equation (
1.4d
) with
u^{\varepsilon}
and using integration by parts and (
1.4e
), we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|u^{\varepsilon}(t)\|^{2}_{L^{2}}=-\nu\|\nabla u^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}-\int_{\Omega}u^{\varepsilon}\rho^{\varepsilon}(\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}+\nabla\Phi_{W})\,dx.

By
summing up the above equalities and recalling the definition of electrochemical potentials (
2.1
), we obtain (
2.9
).

To prove (
2.10
), it suffices to estimate the right hand side of (
2.9
). Indeed
we deduce from Lemma
2.1
that

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\sum^{2}_{i=1}\int_{\Omega}u^{\varepsilon}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}\nabla\log\Gamma_{i}\,dx-\int_{\Omega}u^{\varepsilon}\rho^{\varepsilon}\nabla\Phi_{W}\,dx+\sum^{2}_{i=1}D_{i}\int_{\Omega}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}\nabla\mu^{\varepsilon}_{i}\nabla\mu^{*}_{i}\,dx

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\|u^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\sum^{2}_{i=1}D_{i}\int_{\Omega}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}|\nabla\mu^{\varepsilon}_{i}|^{2}\,dx+M(\Lambda,W,\gamma_{i},z_{i},D_{i})

\displaystyle\leq E^{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{2}\sum^{2}_{i=1}D_{i}\int_{\Omega}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}|\nabla\mu^{\varepsilon}_{i}|^{2}\,dx+M(\Lambda,W,\gamma_{i},z_{i},D_{i}).

Then (
2.10
) follows from Gronwall’s inequality.

Recalling
D^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\min\{D_{1},D_{2}\}
, we deduce
from (
1.2
), (
1.3
), (
1.4c
), Lemma
2.1
that

\displaystyle\sum^{2}_{i=1}D_{i}

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}|\nabla\mu^{\varepsilon}_{i}|^{2}\,dx\geq D^{*}\sum^{2}_{i=1}\int_{\Omega}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}|\nabla\mu^{\varepsilon}_{i}|^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle=D^{*}\sum^{2}_{i=1}\int_{\Omega}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}\bigl|\frac{\nabla c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}+z_{i}(\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}+\nabla\Phi_{W})\bigr|^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle=D^{*}\sum^{2}_{i=1}\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\nabla c^{\varepsilon}_{i}|^{2}}{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}\,dx+2D^{*}\int_{\Omega}\nabla\rho^{\varepsilon}(\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}+\nabla\Phi_{W})\,dx

\displaystyle\quad+D^{*}\int_{\Omega}(z_{1}^{2}c_{1}^{\varepsilon}+z_{2}^{2}c_{2}^{\varepsilon})|\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}+\nabla\Phi_{W}|^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle\geq 2D^{*}\|\frac{\rho^{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+D^{*}(z_{1}^{2}+z_{2}^{2})\lambda\|\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}+\nabla\Phi_{W}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\frac{D^{*}}{\Lambda}\sum_{i=1}^{2}\|\nabla c^{\varepsilon}_{i}\|_{L^{2}}^{2},

which leads to (
2.11
). This completes the proof of Lemma
2.3
.
∎

As we mentioned in the introduction, the global wellposedness of 2-D NPNS system with Dirichlet boundary conditions (
1.5
)
was settled in
[
4
]
(see Theorem 9). While it follows from Theorem 3 of
[
5
]
that
the 3-D binary NPNS system with Dirichlet boundary conditions (
1.5
) has a unique strong solution on
[0,T]

as long as
U(T)=\int^{T}_{0}\|u^{\varepsilon}\|^{4}_{V}dt<\infty
. Based on this criterion, we are able to derive an
\varepsilon
-independent lower bound
for the lifespan of the
\varepsilon
-depending NPNS system (
1.4
)-(
1.5
). Precisely, we have the following Theorem.

Theorem 2.1
.

Under the assumption (
1.11
), one has

•

2-D system (
1.4
) with boundary conditions (
1.5
) has a unique global strong solution and there exists a positive constant
M
so that for any
T\geq 0
,

(2.12)

\|u^{\varepsilon}\|_{V}^{2}+\int_{0}^{T}\|Au^{\varepsilon}\|_{H}^{2}dt\leq M(\|u^{\varepsilon}(0)\|_{V}^{2}+E^{\varepsilon}(0))e^{ME^{\varepsilon}(0)}.

•

If we assume in addition that
\|u^{\varepsilon}(0)\|_{V}^{2}+E^{\varepsilon}(0)\leq M_{in}<+\infty
. Then there exists an
\varepsilon
-independent
T>0,
so that the 3-D NPNS system (
1.4
) with boundary conditions (
1.5
) has a unique strong solution on
[0,T]
, and there exists an increasing function
h(a,b):\mathbb{R^{+}}\times\mathbb{R^{+}}\to\mathbb{R^{+}}
, so that

(2.13)

\|u^{\varepsilon}\|_{V}^{2}+\int_{0}^{T}\|Au^{\varepsilon}\|_{H}^{2}dt\leq h(\|u^{\varepsilon}(0)\|_{V}^{2},E^{\varepsilon}(0)).

Proof.

Based on Theorem 3 of
[
5
]
, it suffice to deal with the
a priori

estimate of
\|u^{\varepsilon}\|_{V}
. By taking
L^{2}
inner product of (
1.4c
) with
Au^{\varepsilon}
and using standard estimates for the Navier-Stokes equations in
[
3
]
, Lemma
2.1
and the assumption (
1.11
), we find

(2.14)

\frac{d}{dt}\|u^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{V}+\nu\|Au^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{H}\leq M(\Lambda,z_{i})(\|u^{\varepsilon}\|^{4}_{V}+\|\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}+\nabla\Phi_{W}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}),\qquad d=2,

and

(2.15)

\frac{d}{dt}\|u^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{V}+\nu\|Au^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{H}\leq M(\Lambda,z_{i})(\|u^{\varepsilon}\|^{6}_{V}+\|\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}+\nabla\Phi_{W}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}),\qquad d=3.

Thus, together with estimate (
2.10
) and (
2.11
), (
2.14
) gives rise to (
2.12
), (
2.15
) implies the existence of
T
so that (
2.13
) holds.
∎

Remark 2.2
.

It is easy to deduce the local wellposedness of the limit system (
1.6a
)-(
1.6e
) with boundary condition (
1.6f
).
As a consequence, we can choose
T>0
so that both the
\varepsilon
-depending NPNS systems and the limit system have a unique strong solution on the time interval
[0,T]
.

Next Lemma is about the convexity of the energy function.

Lemma 2.4
.

For arbitrary
0<m\leq s\leq M
, we have

\frac{1}{2M}(s-1)^{2}\leq\varphi(s)\leq\frac{1}{2m}(s-1)^{2}.

Proof.

By using Taylor expansion and the fact that
\varphi{{}^{\prime\prime}}(s)=\frac{1}{s}
, for arbitrary
0<s<M
, we have

\varphi(s)=\varphi(1)+\varphi{{}^{\prime}}(1)(s-1)+\frac{\varphi{{}^{\prime\prime}}(\theta)}{2}(s-1)^{2}\geq\frac{1}{2M}(s-1)^{2},

where
\theta
is a number between
1
and
s
. This completes the proof of the second inequality. The first inequality follows
along the same line.
∎

Remark 2.3
.

In particular, by taking
s=\frac{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}{c_{i}}
, we deduce from Lemmas
2.4
and
2.1
that

\int_{\Omega}c_{i}\varphi\bigl(\frac{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}{c_{i}}\bigr)\,dx\approx\|c^{\varepsilon}_{i}-c_{i}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}.

3.
The Convergence in
L_{T}^{\infty}(L^{2})

In this section, we shall use modulated energy estimate to prove Theorem
1.1
, which will be
based on the following modulated energy functional:

(3.1)

H^{\varepsilon}(t)\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\sum^{2}_{i=1}\int_{\Omega}c_{i}\varphi\bigl(\frac{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}{c_{i}}\bigr)(t)\,dx+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\|\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}(t)\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\|(u^{\varepsilon}-u)(t)\|^{2}_{L^{2}},

where
(c^{\varepsilon}_{1},c^{\varepsilon}_{2},\psi^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon})
,
(c_{1},c_{2},\psi,u)
are smooth enough solutions of NPNS system (
1.4
-
1.5
) and the limit system (
1.6
) on
[0,T]
respectively.
We construct the energy dissipation functional
\Theta^{\varepsilon}(t)
as follows:

(3.2)

\begin{split}\Theta^{\varepsilon}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}&\sum^{2}_{i=1}D_{i}\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\nabla c^{\varepsilon}_{i}-\nabla c_{i}|^{2}}{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}\,dx+\sum^{2}_{i=1}z^{2}_{i}D_{i}\int_{\Omega}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}|\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}-\nabla\psi|^{2}\,dx\\
&+{D^{*}}\bigl\|\frac{\rho^{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon}\bigr\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\nu\|(\nabla u^{\varepsilon}-\nabla u)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.\end{split}

The main result states as follows:

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
(c^{\varepsilon}_{1},c^{\varepsilon}_{2},\psi^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon})
,
(c_{1},c_{2},\psi,u)
be smooth enough solutions of NPNS system (
1.4
-
1.5
) and the limiting system (
1.6
) on
[0,T]
respectively. Then for any
t\in[0,T]
: one has

(3.3)

\begin{split}\frac{d}{dt}&H^{\varepsilon}(t)+\frac{D_{2}}{4D_{1}}\Theta^{\varepsilon}(t)\\
\leq&M\bigl(1+\|\nabla u\|_{L^{\infty}}+\sum_{i=1}^{2}\|\nabla c_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}+\|\nabla\psi\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}+\|\nabla\Phi_{W}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\bigr)H^{\varepsilon}(t)\\
&+M\varepsilon^{2}\|\Delta\psi\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\int_{\Omega}\psi(\partial_{t}\rho^{\varepsilon}+u^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\rho^{\varepsilon})\,dx.\end{split}

Here and in the rest of this paper, we always denote
M
to be a positive constant which depends on

\nu,\lambda,\Lambda,z_{i}
and
D_{i}
for
i=1,2
, unless otherwise stated, and which may vary from line to line.

Proof.

We shall divide the proof of Proposition
3.1
into the following steps:

Step 1.
The derivation of the differential equality.

Observing that

\displaystyle c_{i}\varphi\bigl(\frac{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}{c_{i}}\bigr)

\displaystyle=c_{i}\bigl(\frac{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}{c_{i}}\log\frac{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}{c_{i}}-\frac{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}{c_{i}}+1\bigr)=c^{\varepsilon}_{i}\log c^{\varepsilon}_{i}-c^{\varepsilon}_{i}\log c_{i}-c^{\varepsilon}_{i}+c_{i}

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{i}\varphi(\frac{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}{\Gamma_{i}})-c^{\varepsilon}_{i}(\log c_{i}-\log\Gamma_{i})+c_{i}-\Gamma_{i},

in view of (
2.8
) and (
3.1
), we have

(3.4)

H^{\varepsilon}(t)=E^{\varepsilon}(t)-\sum^{2}_{i=1}\int_{\Omega}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}\bigl(\log c_{i}-\log\Gamma_{i}\bigr)\,dx+\sum^{2}_{i=1}\int_{\Omega}\bigl(c_{i}-\Gamma_{i}\bigr)\,dx+\frac{1}{2}\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}}-\int_{\Omega}u^{\varepsilon}\cdot u\,dx.

Below let us calculate the time derivative of the right-hand side of (
3.4
).
Indeed it follows from (
2.9
) that

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}E^{\varepsilon}(t)=

\displaystyle-\nu\|\nabla u^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}-\sum^{2}_{i=1}D_{i}\int_{\Omega}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}|\nabla\mu^{\varepsilon}_{i}|^{2}\,dx-\sum^{2}_{i=1}\int_{\Omega}u^{\varepsilon}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}\nabla\log\Gamma_{i}\,dx

\displaystyle-\int_{\Omega}u^{\varepsilon}\rho^{\varepsilon}\nabla\Phi_{W}\,dx+\sum^{2}_{i=1}D_{i}\int_{\Omega}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}\nabla\mu^{\varepsilon}_{i}\nabla(\log\Gamma_{i}+z_{i}\Phi_{W})\,dx.

While by virtue of (
2.2
), (
2.3
) and
\frac{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}{c_{i}}|_{\partial\Omega}=1,
we get,
by using integration by parts, that

\displaystyle-\frac{d}{dt}

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}(\log c_{i}-\log\Gamma_{i})\,dx=-\int_{\Omega}\partial_{t}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}(\log c_{i}-\log\Gamma_{i})\,\,dx-\int_{\Omega}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}\frac{\partial_{t}c_{i}}{c_{i}}\,dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{\Omega}\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits(D_{i}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}\nabla\mu^{\varepsilon}_{i}-u^{\varepsilon}c^{\varepsilon}_{i})(\log c_{i}-\log\Gamma_{i})\,dx-\int_{\Omega}\frac{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}{c_{i}}\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits(D_{i}c_{i}\nabla\mu_{i}-uc_{i})\,dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D_{i}\int_{\Omega}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}\nabla\mu^{\varepsilon}_{i}\bigl(\frac{\nabla c_{i}}{c_{i}}-\nabla\log\Gamma_{i}\bigr)\,dx-\int_{\Omega}u^{\varepsilon}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}\bigl(\frac{\nabla c_{i}}{c_{i}}-\nabla\log\Gamma_{i}\bigr)\,dx

\displaystyle+D_{i}\int_{\Omega}\nabla\bigl(\frac{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}{c_{i}}\bigr)c_{i}\nabla\mu_{i}\,dx-D_{i}\int_{\Omega}\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits(c_{i}\nabla\mu_{i})\,dx+\int_{\Omega}\frac{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}{c_{i}}u\cdot\nabla c_{i}\,dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D_{i}\int_{\Omega}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}\nabla\mu^{\varepsilon}_{i}\frac{\nabla c_{i}}{c_{i}}\,dx+D_{i}\int_{\Omega}\nabla\bigl(\frac{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}{c_{i}}\bigr)c_{i}\nabla\mu_{i}\,dx-\int_{\Omega}(u^{\varepsilon}-u)\frac{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}{c_{i}}\nabla c_{i}\,dx

\displaystyle-D_{i}\int_{\Omega}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}\nabla\mu^{\varepsilon}_{i}\nabla\log\Gamma_{i}\,dx+\int_{\Omega}u^{\varepsilon}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}\nabla\log\Gamma_{i}\,dx-D_{i}\int_{\Omega}\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits(c_{i}\nabla\mu_{i})\,dx.

Thanks to (
2.3
), one has

\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\Omega}c_{i}\,dx=D_{i}\int_{\Omega}\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits(c_{i}\nabla\mu_{i})\,dx.

Whereas due to
\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits u=0
and
u|_{\partial\Omega}=0,
we get, by using energy method to equation (
1.6d
), that

\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}}=-\nu\|\nabla u\|^{2}_{L^{2}}.

Finally thanks to (
1.4e
) and (
1.6e
), we get, by using integration by parts, that

\displaystyle-\frac{d}{dt}

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}u^{\varepsilon}u\,dx=-\int_{\Omega}\partial_{t}u^{\varepsilon}u\,dx-\int_{\Omega}u^{\varepsilon}\partial_{t}u\,dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{\Omega}\bigl(\nu\Delta u^{\varepsilon}-u^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla u^{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon^{2}\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits(\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}\otimes\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon})-\rho^{\varepsilon}\nabla\Phi_{W}\bigr)\cdot u\,dx

\displaystyle-\int_{\Omega}u^{\varepsilon}\cdot(\nu\Delta u-u\cdot\nabla u)\,dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\nu\int_{\Omega}\nabla u^{\varepsilon}:\nabla u\,dx+\int_{\Omega}(u^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla u^{\varepsilon})\cdot u\,dx+\int_{\Omega}(u\cdot\nabla u)\cdot u^{\varepsilon}\,dx

\displaystyle+\varepsilon^{2}\int_{\Omega}\nabla u:\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}\otimes\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}\,dx+\int_{\Omega}u\rho^{\varepsilon}\nabla\Phi_{W}\,dx.

Thanks to (
3.4
), we get,
by summarizing the above equalities, that

(3.5)

\frac{d}{dt}H^{\varepsilon}(t)=-\nu\|\nabla(u^{\varepsilon}-u)\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3},

where

(3.6)

\begin{split}I_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}&\int_{\Omega}(u^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla u^{\varepsilon})\cdot u\,dx+\int_{\Omega}(u\cdot\nabla u)\cdot u^{\varepsilon}\,dx,\\
I_{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}&\varepsilon^{2}\int_{\Omega}\nabla u:\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}\otimes\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}\,dx-\sum^{2}_{i=1}\int_{\Omega}(u^{\varepsilon}-u)\bigl(\frac{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}{c_{i}}-1\bigr)\nabla c_{i}\,dx\\
&-\int_{\Omega}(u^{\varepsilon}-u)\rho^{\varepsilon}\nabla\Phi_{W}\,dx,\\
I_{3}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}&-\sum^{2}_{i=1}D_{i}\int_{\Omega}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}|\nabla\mu^{\varepsilon}_{i}|^{2}\,dx+\sum^{2}_{i=1}D_{i}\int_{\Omega}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}\nabla\mu^{\varepsilon}_{i}\frac{\nabla c_{i}}{c_{i}}\,dx\\
&+\sum^{2}_{i=1}D_{i}\int_{\Omega}\nabla\Bigl(\frac{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}{c_{i}}\Bigr)c_{i}\nabla\mu_{i}\,dx+\sum^{2}_{i=1}z_{i}D_{i}\int_{\Omega}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}\nabla\mu^{\varepsilon}_{i}\cdot\nabla\Phi_{W}\,dx.\end{split}

Step 2.
The estimates of
I_{1}
to
I_{2}.

We first get, by using
\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits u^{\varepsilon}=\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits u=0
and the homogeneous
boundary conditions of
u^{\varepsilon}
and
u,
that

\displaystyle\begin{split}I_{1}&=\int_{\Omega}(u^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla u^{\varepsilon})\cdot u\,dx+\int_{\Omega}(u\cdot\nabla u)\cdot u^{\varepsilon}\,dx=\int_{\Omega}\big((u^{\varepsilon}-u)\cdot\nabla u\big)\cdot(u-u^{\varepsilon})\,dx,\end{split}

from which, we infer

(3.7)

|I_{1}|\leq\|\nabla u\|_{L^{\infty}}\|u^{\varepsilon}-u\|^{2}_{L^{2}}.

Similarly, in view of
\rho^{\varepsilon}=\sum_{i=1}^{2}z_{i}(c^{\varepsilon}_{i}-c_{i})
, one has

(3.8)

\begin{split}|I_{2}|\leq&\varepsilon^{2}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{2}\|\nabla c_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\frac{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}{c_{i}}-1\|_{L^{2}}+\|\nabla\Phi_{W}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\rho^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}}\bigr)\|u^{\varepsilon}-u\|_{L^{2}}\\
\leq&M\bigl(\|\nabla u\|_{L^{\infty}}+\sum_{i=1}^{2}\|\nabla c_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\nabla\Phi_{W}\|_{L^{\infty}}\bigr)H^{\varepsilon}.\end{split}

Step 3.
The estimates of
I_{3}.

In view of (
2.1
), we write

(3.9)

\begin{split}I_{3}=-\sum^{2}_{i=1}D_{i}\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\nabla c^{\varepsilon}_{i}-\nabla c_{i}|^{2}}{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}\,dx-\sum^{2}_{i=1}z_{i}^{2}D_{i}\int_{\Omega}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}|\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}-\nabla\psi|^{2}\,dx+I_{31}+I_{32}+I_{33},\ \end{split}

where we denote
I_{31}
,
I_{32}
and
I_{33}
respectively by

\displaystyle I_{31}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}

\displaystyle-\sum^{2}_{i=1}D_{i}\int_{\Omega}\frac{2\nabla c^{\varepsilon}_{i}\cdot\nabla c_{i}-|\nabla c_{i}|^{2}}{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}\,dx

\displaystyle+\sum^{2}_{i=1}D_{i}\bigl(\int_{\Omega}\frac{\nabla c^{\varepsilon}_{i}\cdot\nabla c_{i}}{c_{i}}\,dx+\int_{\Omega}(\nabla c^{\varepsilon}_{i}-\frac{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}{c_{i}}\nabla c_{i})\frac{\nabla c_{i}}{c_{i}}\,dx\bigr),

\displaystyle I_{32}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}

\displaystyle\sum^{2}_{i=1}z_{i}D_{i}\int_{\Omega}\frac{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}{c_{i}}\nabla c_{i}(\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}-\nabla\psi)\,dx-\sum^{2}_{i=1}z_{i}D_{i}\int_{\Omega}\nabla c^{\varepsilon}_{i}(\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}-\nabla\psi)\,dx

\displaystyle-\sum^{2}_{i=1}z_{i}D_{i}\int_{\Omega}\bigl(\nabla c^{\varepsilon}_{i}+z_{i}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}(\nabla\psi+\nabla\Phi_{W})\bigr)(\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}+\nabla\Phi_{W})\,dx

\displaystyle-\sum^{2}_{i=1}z_{i}^{2}D_{i}\int_{\Omega}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}(\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}-\nabla\psi)(\nabla\psi+\nabla\Phi_{W})\,dx,

\displaystyle I_{33}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}

\displaystyle\sum^{2}_{i=1}z_{i}D_{i}\int_{\Omega}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}\nabla\mu^{\varepsilon}_{i}\cdot\nabla\Phi_{W}\,dx.

Step 3.1
The estimates of
I_{31}.

The estimate of
I_{31}
is straightforward. Notice that

\frac{1}{c_{i}}-\frac{1}{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}=\bigl(\frac{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}{c_{i}}-1\bigr)\frac{1}{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}\quad\hbox{and}\quad\frac{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}{c_{i}^{2}}-\frac{1}{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}=\bigl(\frac{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}{c_{i}}+1\bigr)\bigl(\frac{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}{c_{i}}-1\bigr)\frac{1}{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}},

we write

\displaystyle I_{31}

\displaystyle=2\sum^{2}_{i=1}D_{i}\int_{\Omega}\nabla c^{\varepsilon}_{i}\cdot\nabla c_{i}\bigl(\frac{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}{c_{i}}-1\bigr)\frac{1}{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}\,dx-\sum^{2}_{i=1}D_{i}\int_{\Omega}\bigl(\frac{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}{c_{i}}+1\bigr)|\nabla c_{i}|^{2}\bigl(\frac{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}{c_{i}}-1\bigr)\frac{1}{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}\,dx

\displaystyle=2\sum^{2}_{i=1}D_{i}\int_{\Omega}(\nabla c^{\varepsilon}_{i}-\nabla c_{i})\cdot\nabla c_{i}\bigl(\frac{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}{c_{i}}-1\bigr)\frac{1}{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}\,dx-\sum^{2}_{i=1}D_{i}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla c_{i}|^{2}\bigl(\frac{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}{c_{i}}-1\bigr)^{2}\frac{1}{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}\,dx.

Then we get, by using Lemma
2.1
, Lemma
2.4
and Hölder’s inequality, that for any
\delta>0,

\displaystyle|I_{31}|

\displaystyle\leq\delta\sum_{i=1}^{2}D_{i}\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\nabla c^{\varepsilon}_{i}-\nabla c_{i}|^{2}}{c_{i}^{\varepsilon}}\,dx+\frac{2}{\delta}\sum^{2}_{i=1}D_{i}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla c_{i}|^{2}\bigl(\frac{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}{c_{i}}-1\bigr)^{2}\frac{1}{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}\,dx

\displaystyle\leq\delta\Theta^{\varepsilon}+\frac{M}{\delta}\bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{2}\|\nabla c_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\bigr)H^{\varepsilon}.

Step 3.2
The estimates of
I_{32}.

We split
I_{32}
further into the following two parts:

\displaystyle I_{32}=

\displaystyle J_{1}+J_{2}\quad\hbox{with}\quad

\displaystyle J_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}

\displaystyle\sum^{2}_{i=1}z_{i}D_{i}\int_{\Omega}\frac{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}{c_{i}}\nabla c_{i}(\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}-\nabla\psi)\,dx-\sum^{2}_{i=1}z_{i}D_{i}\int_{\Omega}\nabla c^{\varepsilon}_{i}(\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}-\nabla\psi)\,dx,

\displaystyle J_{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}

\displaystyle-\sum^{2}_{i=1}z_{i}D_{i}\int_{\Omega}\big(\nabla c^{\varepsilon}_{i}+z_{i}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}(\nabla\psi+\nabla\Phi_{W})\big)(\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}+\nabla\Phi_{W})\,dx

\displaystyle-\sum^{2}_{i=1}z_{i}^{2}D_{i}\int_{\Omega}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}(\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}-\nabla\psi)(\nabla\psi+\nabla\Phi_{W})\,dx.

It is easy to observe that

\displaystyle J_{1}=\sum^{2}_{i=1}z_{i}D_{i}\int_{\Omega}\bigl(\frac{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}{c_{i}}-1\bigr)\nabla c_{i}(\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}-\nabla\psi)\,dx-\sum^{2}_{i=1}z_{i}D_{i}\int_{\Omega}(\nabla c^{\varepsilon}_{i}-\nabla c_{i})(\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}-\nabla\psi)\,dx,

so that one has

\displaystyle|J_{1}|

\displaystyle\leq\bigl(\delta+\frac{1}{2}\bigr)\sum_{i=1}^{2}D_{i}z_{i}^{2}\int_{\Omega}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}|\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}-\nabla\psi|^{2}\,dx+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{2}D_{i}\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\nabla c^{\varepsilon}_{i}-\nabla c_{i}|^{2}}{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}\,dx

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{M}{\delta}\sum_{i=1}^{2}\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\nabla c_{i}|^{2}}{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}\bigl(\frac{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}{c_{i}}-1\bigr)^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle\leq\bigl(\delta+\frac{1}{2}\bigr)\Theta^{\varepsilon}+\frac{M}{\delta}\bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{2}\|\nabla c_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\bigr)H^{\varepsilon}.

The estimate of the rest part in
I_{32}
relies on the evolution equation of
\rho^{\varepsilon}
. We first write

\displaystyle J_{2}

\displaystyle=-\int_{\Omega}(\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}-\nabla\psi)\Big(z_{1}D_{1}\nabla c^{\varepsilon}_{1}+z_{2}D_{2}\nabla c^{\varepsilon}_{2}+(z_{1}^{2}D_{1}c^{\varepsilon}_{1}+z_{2}^{2}D_{2}c^{\varepsilon}_{2})(\nabla\psi+\nabla\Phi_{W})\Big)\,dx

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{\Omega}(\nabla\psi+\nabla\Phi_{W})\Big(z_{1}D_{1}\nabla c^{\varepsilon}_{1}+z_{2}D_{2}\nabla c^{\varepsilon}_{2}+(z_{1}^{2}D_{1}c^{\varepsilon}_{1}+z_{2}^{2}D_{2}c^{\varepsilon}_{2})(\nabla\psi+\nabla\Phi_{W})\Big)\,dx

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{\Omega}(\nabla\psi+\nabla\Phi_{W})(\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}-\nabla\psi)(z_{1}^{2}D_{1}c^{\varepsilon}_{1}+z_{2}^{2}D_{2}c^{\varepsilon}_{2})\,dx.

As
\rho^{\varepsilon}=z_{1}c_{1}^{\varepsilon}+z_{2}c_{2}^{\varepsilon},
we have

\displaystyle J_{2}

\displaystyle=-\int_{\Omega}(\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}-\nabla\psi)\Big(z_{1}(D_{1}-D_{2})\nabla c^{\varepsilon}_{1}+D_{2}\nabla\rho^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\big(z_{1}(z_{1}D_{1}-z_{2}D_{2})c^{\varepsilon}_{1}+z_{2}D_{2}\rho^{\varepsilon}\big)(\nabla\psi+\nabla\Phi_{W})\Big)\,dx

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{\Omega}(\nabla\psi+\nabla\Phi_{W})\Big(z_{1}D_{1}\nabla c^{\varepsilon}_{1}+z_{2}D_{2}\nabla c^{\varepsilon}_{2}+(z_{1}^{2}D_{1}c^{\varepsilon}_{1}+z_{2}^{2}D_{2}c^{\varepsilon}_{2})(\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}+\nabla\Phi_{W})\Big)\,dx,

which together with the
\psi
equation (
1.8
) ensures that

\displaystyle J_{2}

\displaystyle=-\int_{\Omega}(\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}-\nabla\psi)\Big(z_{1}(D_{1}-D_{2})(\nabla c^{\varepsilon}_{1}-\nabla c_{1})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\big(z_{1}(z_{1}D_{1}-z_{2}D_{2})c_{1}(\frac{c^{\varepsilon}_{1}}{c_{1}}-1)\big)(\nabla\psi+\nabla\Phi_{W})\Big)\,dx

\displaystyle\quad-D_{2}\int_{\Omega}(\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}-\nabla\psi)\nabla\rho^{\varepsilon}\,dx-z_{2}D_{2}\int_{\Omega}(\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}-\nabla\psi)\rho^{\varepsilon}(\nabla\psi+\nabla\Phi_{W})\,dx

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{\Omega}(\nabla\psi+\nabla\Phi_{W})\big(z_{1}D_{1}c^{\varepsilon}_{1}\nabla\mu^{\varepsilon}_{1}+z_{2}D_{2}c^{\varepsilon}_{2}\nabla\mu^{\varepsilon}_{2}\big)\,dx.

For the first line in
J_{2}
, we get, by applying Hölder’s inequality, that

\displaystyle\bigl|\int_{\Omega}(\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}-\nabla\psi)\big(z_{1}(D_{1}-D_{2})(\nabla c^{\varepsilon}_{1}-\nabla c_{1})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad+\big(z_{1}(z_{1}D_{1}-z_{2}D_{2})c_{1}(\frac{c^{\varepsilon}_{1}}{c_{1}}-1)\big)(\nabla\psi+\nabla\Phi_{W})\big)\,dx\bigr|

\displaystyle\leq\bigl(1-\frac{D_{2}}{D_{1}}\bigr)D_{1}\Bigl(\bigl(\delta+\frac{1}{2}\bigr)z_{1}^{2}\int_{\Omega}c_{1}^{\varepsilon}|\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}-\nabla\psi|^{2}\,dx+\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\nabla c^{\varepsilon}_{1}-\nabla c_{1}|^{2}}{c_{1}^{\varepsilon}}\,dx\Bigr)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{M}{\delta}\bigl(\|\nabla\psi\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}+\|\nabla\Phi_{W}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\bigr)\|c_{1}^{\varepsilon}-c_{1}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\bigl(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{D_{2}}{2D_{1}}\bigr)\Theta^{\varepsilon}+\delta\Theta^{\varepsilon}+\frac{M}{\delta}\bigl(\|\nabla\psi\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}+\|\nabla\Phi_{W}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\bigr)H^{\varepsilon}.

For the second line in
J_{2}
, by using integration by parts and the equation (
1.4c
), we obtain

\displaystyle-D_{2}\int_{\Omega}(\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}-\nabla\psi)\nabla\rho^{\varepsilon}\,dx-z_{2}D_{2}\int_{\Omega}(\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}-\nabla\psi)\rho^{\varepsilon}(\nabla\psi+\nabla\Phi_{W})\,dx

\displaystyle\leq-D_{2}\|\frac{\rho^{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+D_{2}\|\Delta\psi\|_{L^{2}}\|\rho^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}}+D_{2}|z_{2}|\bigl(\|\nabla\psi\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\nabla\Phi_{W}\|_{L^{\infty}}\bigr)\|\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}-\nabla\psi\|_{L^{2}}\|\rho^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq-D_{2}\|\frac{\rho^{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\delta D_{2}\|\frac{\rho^{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\delta D_{2}z_{2}^{2}\int_{\Omega}c_{2}^{\varepsilon}|\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}-\nabla\psi|^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{M}{\delta}\bigl(\|\nabla\psi\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}+\|\nabla\Phi_{W}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\bigr)H^{\varepsilon}+\frac{M}{\delta}\varepsilon^{2}\|\Delta\psi\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

Finally by virtue of (
2.2
), we find

\displaystyle-\int_{\Omega}(\nabla\psi+\nabla\Phi_{W})\Big(z_{1}D_{1}c^{\varepsilon}_{1}\nabla\mu^{\varepsilon}_{1}+z_{2}D_{2}c^{\varepsilon}_{2}\nabla\mu^{\varepsilon}_{2}\Big)\,dx+I_{33}

\displaystyle=-\int_{\Omega}\nabla\psi\Big(z_{1}D_{1}c^{\varepsilon}_{1}\nabla\mu^{\varepsilon}_{1}+z_{2}D_{2}c^{\varepsilon}_{2}\nabla\mu^{\varepsilon}_{2}\Big)\,dx

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\psi\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits\Big(z_{1}D_{1}c^{\varepsilon}_{1}\nabla\mu^{\varepsilon}_{1}+z_{2}D_{2}c^{\varepsilon}_{2}\nabla\mu^{\varepsilon}_{2}\Big)\,dx

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\psi(\partial_{t}\rho^{\varepsilon}+u^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\rho^{\varepsilon})\,dx.

By substituting the above estimates into (
3.9
), we achieve

(3.10)

\begin{split}I_{3}\leq&-\sum^{2}_{i=1}D_{i}\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\nabla c^{\varepsilon}_{i}-\nabla c_{i}|^{2}}{c^{\varepsilon}_{i}}\,dx-\sum^{2}_{i=1}z_{i}^{2}D_{i}\int_{\Omega}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}|\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}-\nabla\psi|^{2}\,dx-D_{2}\|\frac{\rho^{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\\
&+\bigl(1+4\delta-\frac{D_{2}}{2D_{1}}\bigr)\Theta^{\varepsilon}+\frac{M}{\delta}\bigl(1+\sum_{i=1}^{2}\|\nabla c_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}+\|\nabla\psi\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}+\|\nabla\Phi_{W}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\bigr)H^{\varepsilon}\\
&+M\varepsilon^{2}\|\Delta\psi\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\int_{\Omega}\psi(\partial_{t}\rho^{\varepsilon}+u^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\rho^{\varepsilon})\,dx.\end{split}

By inserting the estimates (
3.7
), (
3.8
) and (
3.10
) into (
3.5
) and then
taking
\delta=\frac{D_{2}}{16D_{1}},
we arrive at (
3.3
). This completes the proof of Proposition
3.1
.
∎

Now we are in a position to complete the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

Firstly, together with (
1.12a
-
1.12b
) and
\psi^{\varepsilon}(0)=0
, Theorem
2.1
implies the existence of
T
so that the systems (
1.4
) and (
1.6
) have unique strong solutions on
[0,T].
Since the initial data is “well-prepared”, by virtue of (
1.4c
), we get, by using integration by parts, that

(3.11)

\begin{split}\int_{0}^{t}&\int_{\Omega}\psi(\partial_{t}\rho^{\varepsilon}+u^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\rho^{\varepsilon})\,dxdt\\
=&\int_{\Omega}\psi\rho^{\varepsilon}\,dx\big|^{t}_{0}-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\partial_{t}\psi\rho^{\varepsilon}\,dxdt-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}u^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\psi\rho^{\varepsilon}\,dxdt\\
=&\varepsilon^{2}\int_{\Omega}\nabla\psi(t)\cdot\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}(t)\,dx-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}(\partial_{t}\psi+u^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\psi)\rho^{\varepsilon}\,dxdt\\
\leq&\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{4}\|\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}\|\nabla\psi(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\frac{D_{2}}{8D_{1}}D^{*}\bigl\|\frac{\rho^{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon}\bigr\|_{L^{2}_{t}(L^{2})}^{2}\\
&+M\varepsilon^{2}\bigl(\|\partial_{t}\psi\|_{L^{2}_{t}(L^{2})}^{2}+\|\nabla\psi\|_{L^{2}_{t}(L^{\infty})}^{2}\|u^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}(L^{2})}^{2}\bigr).\end{split}

Observing that
\|u^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}(L^{2})}^{2}\lesssim 1,

by integrating (
3.3
) over
[0,t]
and then inserting (
3.11
) to the resulting inequality, we find

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}

\displaystyle H^{\varepsilon}(t)+\frac{D_{2}}{8D_{1}}\int_{0}^{t}\Theta^{\varepsilon}(t^{\prime})\,dt^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle H^{\varepsilon}(0)+M\varepsilon^{2}\bigl(\|\nabla\psi\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{2})}^{2}+\|\partial_{t}\psi\|_{L^{2}_{T}(L^{2})}^{2}+\|\nabla\psi\|_{L^{2}_{T}(L^{\infty})}^{2}+\|\Delta\psi\|_{L^{2}_{T}(L^{2})}^{2}\bigr)

\displaystyle+M\int_{0}^{t}\bigl(1+\|\nabla u\|_{L^{\infty}}+\sum_{i=1}^{2}\|\nabla c_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}+\|\nabla\psi\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}+\|\nabla\Phi_{W}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\bigr)H^{\varepsilon}(t^{\prime})\,dt^{\prime}.

By using Gronwall’s inequality, we achieve

(3.12)

\begin{split}H^{\varepsilon}(t)&+\frac{D_{2}}{16D_{1}}\int_{0}^{t}\Theta^{\varepsilon}(t^{\prime})\,dt^{\prime}\\
\leq&\Bigl(H^{\varepsilon}(0)+M\varepsilon^{2}\bigl(\|\nabla\psi\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{2})}^{2}+\|\partial_{t}\psi\|_{L^{2}_{T}(L^{2})}^{2}+\|\nabla\psi\|_{L^{2}_{T}(L^{\infty})}^{2}+\|\Delta\psi\|_{L^{2}_{T}(L^{2})}^{2}\bigr)\Bigr)\\
&\times\exp\Bigl(M\int_{0}^{t}\bigl(1+\|\nabla u\|_{L^{\infty}}+\sum_{i=1}^{2}\|\nabla c_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}+\|\nabla\psi\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}+\|\nabla\Phi_{W}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\bigr)(t^{\prime})\,dt^{\prime}\Bigr),\end{split}

which together with (
3.1
) and (
3.2
) leads to (
1.13a
-
1.13c
). We thus complete the proof of Theorem
1.1
.
∎

Remark 3.1
.

We remark that it is crucial to deal with the term
\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\psi(\partial_{t}\rho^{\varepsilon}+u^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\rho^{\varepsilon})\,dxdt
as that in (
3.12
).
Indeed in view of (
1.4a
) and (
1.4b
), we have

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle+u^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\rho^{\varepsilon}=D_{2}\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits\bigl(\nabla\rho^{\varepsilon}+z_{2}\rho^{\varepsilon}(\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}+\nabla\Phi_{W})\bigr)

\displaystyle+\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits\bigl((D_{1}-D_{2})z_{1}\nabla c_{1}^{\varepsilon}+(D_{1}z_{1}-D_{2}z_{2})z_{1}c_{1}^{\varepsilon}(\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}+\nabla\Phi_{W})\bigr),

which together with (
1.8
) ensures that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle+u^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\rho^{\varepsilon}=D_{2}\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits\bigl(\nabla\rho^{\varepsilon}+z_{2}\rho^{\varepsilon}(\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}+\nabla\Phi_{W})\bigr)

\displaystyle+\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits\bigl((D_{1}-D_{2})z_{1}\nabla(c_{1}^{\varepsilon}-c_{1})+(D_{1}z_{1}-D_{2}z_{2})z_{1}\bigl(c_{1}^{\varepsilon}(\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}+\nabla\Phi_{W})-c_{1}(\nabla\psi+\nabla\Phi_{W})\bigr).

Yet it is impossible for us to gain
\varepsilon^{2}
for the following term:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}

\displaystyle\psi\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits\bigl((D_{1}-D_{2})z_{1}\nabla(c_{1}^{\varepsilon}-c_{1})

\displaystyle+(D_{1}z_{1}-D_{2}z_{2})z_{1}\bigl(c_{1}^{\varepsilon}(\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}+\nabla\Phi_{W})-c_{1}(\nabla\psi+\nabla\Phi_{W})\bigr)\,dx\,dt^{\prime}.

4.
Analysis of layers

In order to estimate the difference between the solutions of the system (
1.4
) and that of (
1.6
) in stronger norm than
L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{2}),

we need to analysis the initial layer and weak boundary layer. For simplicity, here and in the rest of this paper we always take
\Omega=\mathbb{T}^{d-1}\times[0,1]
for
d=2
or
3
,
and denote the velocity field
u^{\varepsilon}=(v^{\varepsilon},w^{\varepsilon})
, where
v^{\varepsilon}
is the first
d-1
components of
u^{\varepsilon}
. Motivated by
[
15
,
16
]
, we introduce the smooth cut-off functions
f(y)
,
g(y)
as

(4.1)

\quad f(y)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}1,\quad 0\leq y\leq\frac{1}{4},\\
0,\quad\frac{1}{2}\leq y\leq 1,\end{array}\right.\quad\hbox{and}\quad\quad g(y)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}0,\quad 0\leq y\leq\frac{1}{2},\\
1,\quad\frac{3}{4}\leq y\leq 1.\end{array}\right.

And in what follows, we denote

\tau\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\frac{t}{\varepsilon^{2}}
,
\xi\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\frac{y}{\varepsilon}
,
\eta\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\frac{1-y}{\varepsilon}.
Then formally, we expand
(c^{\varepsilon}_{i},\Phi^{\varepsilon},v^{\varepsilon},w^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon})
as follows:

(4.2)

\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}c^{\varepsilon}_{i}(x^{\prime},y,t)=\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}\varepsilon^{k}\Big(c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(k)}(x^{\prime},y,t)+c_{\scriptscriptstyle i,I}^{\scriptscriptstyle(k)}(x^{\prime},y,\tau)+f(y)c_{\scriptscriptstyle i,LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(k)}(x^{\prime},\xi,t)\\
\qquad\qquad\qquad+g(y)c_{\scriptscriptstyle i,RB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(k)}(x^{\prime},\eta,t)+f(y)c_{\scriptscriptstyle i,LM}^{\scriptscriptstyle(k)}(x^{\prime},\xi,\tau)+g(y)c_{\scriptscriptstyle i,RM}^{\scriptscriptstyle(k)}(x^{\prime},\eta,\tau)\Big),\\
\Phi^{\varepsilon}(x^{\prime},y,t)=\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}\varepsilon^{k}\Big(\Phi^{\scriptscriptstyle(k)}(x^{\prime},y,t)+\Phi_{\scriptscriptstyle I}^{\scriptscriptstyle(k)}(x^{\prime},y,\tau)+f(y)\Phi_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(k)}(x^{\prime},\xi,t)\\
\qquad\qquad\qquad+g(y)\Phi_{\scriptscriptstyle RB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(k)}(x^{\prime},\eta,t)+f(y)\Phi_{\scriptscriptstyle LM}^{\scriptscriptstyle(k)}(x^{\prime},\xi,\tau)+g(y)\Phi_{\scriptscriptstyle RM}^{\scriptscriptstyle(k)}(x^{\prime},\eta,\tau)\Big),\\
v^{\varepsilon}(x^{\prime},y,t)=\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}\varepsilon^{k}\Big(v^{\scriptscriptstyle(k)}(x^{\prime},y,t)+v_{\scriptscriptstyle I}^{\scriptscriptstyle(k)}(x^{\prime},y,\tau)+f(y)v_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(k)}(x^{\prime},\xi,t)\\
\qquad\qquad\qquad+g(y)v_{\scriptscriptstyle RB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(k)}(x^{\prime},\eta,t)+f(y)v_{\scriptscriptstyle LM}^{\scriptscriptstyle(k)}(x^{\prime},\xi,\tau)+g(y)v_{\scriptscriptstyle RM}^{\scriptscriptstyle(k)}(x^{\prime},\eta,\tau)\Big),\\
w^{\varepsilon}(x^{\prime},y,t)=\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}\varepsilon^{k}\Big(w^{\scriptscriptstyle(k)}(x^{\prime},y,t)+w_{\scriptscriptstyle I}^{\scriptscriptstyle(k)}(x^{\prime},y,\tau)+f(y)w_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(k)}(x^{\prime},\xi,t)\\
\qquad\qquad\qquad+g(y)w_{\scriptscriptstyle RB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(k)}(x^{\prime},\eta,t)+f(y)w_{\scriptscriptstyle LM}^{\scriptscriptstyle(k)}(x^{\prime},\xi,\tau)+g(y)w_{\scriptscriptstyle RM}^{\scriptscriptstyle(k)}(x^{\prime},\eta,\tau)\Big),\\
p^{\varepsilon}(x^{\prime},y,t)=\sum_{k=-2}^{+\infty}\varepsilon^{k}\Big(p^{\scriptscriptstyle(k)}(x^{\prime},y,t)+p_{\scriptscriptstyle I}^{\scriptscriptstyle(k)}(x^{\prime},y,\tau)+f(y)p_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(k)}(x^{\prime},\xi,t)\\
\qquad\qquad\qquad+g(y)p_{\scriptscriptstyle RB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(k)}(x^{\prime},\eta,t)+f(y)p_{\scriptscriptstyle LM}^{\scriptscriptstyle(k)}(x^{\prime},\xi,\tau)+g(y)p_{\scriptscriptstyle RM}^{\scriptscriptstyle(k)}(x^{\prime},\eta,\tau)\Big),\end{array}\right.

where we set the subscript “I”, “LB”, “RB”, “LM”, “RM” to represent the parts of the solutions in “initial layer”, “boundary layer near
y=0
”, “boundary layer near
y=1
”, “mixed layer near
y=0
”, “mixed layer near
y=1
”, respectively.

By plugging the expansions (
4.2
) into (
1.4
) and comparing the coefficients of
\varepsilon^{k}
, we formally derive the equations
for
\bigl(c_{i}^{(k)},\Phi^{(k)},v^{(k)},w^{(k)},p^{(k)}\bigr)
and the initial, boundary and mixed layers.

Remark 4.1
.

Expansion (
4.2
) is only valid under the assumption of “well-prepared” initial condition and the boundary condition
(\mathbf{EN})
. The expansion of pressure function starts from order of
\varepsilon^{-2}
because we have to balance the term
\Delta u^{\varepsilon}
,
\partial_{t}u^{\varepsilon}
in the boundary and initial layers, respectively.

\bullet
Analysis of inner approximate solutions.

For inner solutions
(c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(k)},\Phi^{\scriptscriptstyle(k)},v^{\scriptscriptstyle(k)},w^{\scriptscriptstyle(k)})
, it’s easy to deduce that
p^{\scriptscriptstyle(-2)}=p^{\scriptscriptstyle(-1)}=\textit{Constant}
(without loss of generality, we take
p^{\scriptscriptstyle(-2)}=p^{\scriptscriptstyle(-1)}=0
) and
(c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},\Phi^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},u^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},p^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)})=(c_{i},\psi+\Phi_{W},u,p)
with boundary conditions (
1.6f
). Thus we have

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
d=2,3,

\lambda\leq c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}(0)\in H^{5}
and
u^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}(0)\in H^{5}
, then system (
1.6
) admits a unique solution
(c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},u^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)})
in
C([0,T_{0}];H^{5})\cap L^{2}(0,T_{0};\dot{H}^{1}\cap\dot{H}^{6})
, where
T_{0}
depends only on
u^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}(0)
and equals to
+\infty
in the case
d=2
.

(c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)},\Phi^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)},u^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)},p^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)})
satisfy the following system

(4.3)

\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\partial_{t}c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}+u^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}\cdot\nabla c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}+u^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}\cdot\nabla c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}=D_{i}\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits(\nabla c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}+z_{i}c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}\nabla\Phi^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}+z_{i}c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}\nabla\Phi^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}),\\
\rho^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}=z_{1}c_{1}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}+z_{2}c_{2}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}=0,\\
\partial_{t}u^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}+u^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}\cdot\nabla u^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}+u^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}\cdot\nabla u^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}-\nu\Delta u^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}+\nabla p^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}=0,\\
\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits u^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}=0.\end{array}\right.

We implement the system (
4.3
) with Dirichlet boundary conditions:

(4.4)

c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}|_{\partial\Omega}=0,\quad u^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}|_{\partial\Omega}=0\quad\hbox{and}\quad\Phi^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}|_{\partial\Omega}=0.

Similar with system (
1.6a
)-(
1.6f
), by using
\rho^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}=0
, we can rewrite the equations of
(c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)},\Phi^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)})
as

\displaystyle\partial_{t}c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}+u^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}\cdot\nabla c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}+u^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}\cdot\nabla c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}=\frac{(z_{1}-z_{2})D_{1}D_{2}}{z_{1}D_{1}-z_{2}D_{2}}\Delta c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)},

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits(\sum_{i=1}^{2}z_{i}D_{i}\nabla c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}+\sum_{i=1}^{2}z_{i}^{2}D_{i}c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}\nabla\Phi^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}+\sum_{i=1}^{2}z_{i}^{2}D_{i}c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}\nabla\Phi^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)})=0.

We have the following Proposition concerning the existence and uniqueness of solution to the system (
4.3
), the proof of which will be omitted.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
d=2,3,

\lambda\leq c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}(0)
and
\bigl(c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}(0),c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}(0),u^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}(0),u^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}(0)\bigr)\in H^{5}
, then system (
4.3
)-(
4.4
) admits a unique solution
(c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)},u^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)})
in
C([0,T_{0}];H^{5})\cap L^{2}(0,T_{0};H^{6})
.

(c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)},\Phi^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)},u^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)},p^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)})
satisfy the following system

(4.5)

\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\partial_{t}c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}+u^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}\cdot\nabla c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}+u^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}\cdot\nabla c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}+u^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}\cdot\nabla c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}=D_{i}\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits(\nabla c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}+z_{i}c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}\nabla\Phi^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)})\\
\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+z_{i}c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}\nabla\Phi^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}+z_{i}c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}\nabla\Phi^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}),\\
\rho^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}=-\Delta\Phi^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},\\
\partial_{t}u^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}+u^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}\cdot\nabla u^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}+u^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}\cdot\nabla u^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}+u^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}\cdot\nabla u^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}-\nu\Delta u^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}+\nabla p^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}=\Delta\Phi^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}\nabla\Phi^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},\\
\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits u^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}=0.\end{array}\right.

To match boundary conditions (
1.5
), We implement the system (
4.5
) with the boundary conditions:

(4.6)

\begin{split}&\qquad c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}(x^{\prime},0,t)=-c_{\scriptscriptstyle i,LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}(x^{\prime},0,t),\quad c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}(x^{\prime},1,t)=-c_{\scriptscriptstyle i,RB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}(x^{\prime},1,t),\\
&\Phi^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}(x^{\prime},0,t)=-\Phi_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}(x^{\prime},0,t),\quad\Phi^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}(x^{\prime},1,t)=-\Phi_{\scriptscriptstyle RB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}(x^{\prime},1,t)\quad\hbox{and}\quad u^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}|_{\partial\Omega}=0,\end{split}

where
(c_{\scriptscriptstyle i,LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)},c_{\scriptscriptstyle i,RB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)},\Phi_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)},\Phi_{\scriptscriptstyle RB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)})
are defined in (
4.10
), (
4.11
). Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{t}c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}+u^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}\cdot\nabla c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}+u^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}\cdot\nabla c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}+u^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}\cdot\nabla c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}-\frac{(z_{1}-z_{2})D_{1}D_{2}}{z_{1}D_{1}-z_{2}D_{2}}\Delta c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}

\displaystyle\qquad=-\frac{D_{i}}{z_{1}D_{1}-z_{2}D_{2}}(\partial_{t}+u^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}\cdot\nabla)\Delta\Phi^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}+\frac{D_{1}D_{2}}{z_{1}D_{1}-z_{2}D_{2}}\Delta\Delta\Phi^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{z_{1}z_{2}D_{1}D_{2}}{z_{i}(z_{1}D_{1}-z_{2}D_{2})}\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits(\Delta\Phi^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}\nabla\Phi^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}),

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{2}z_{i}D_{i}\nabla c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}+\sum_{i=1}^{2}(z_{i}^{2}D_{i}c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)})\nabla\Phi^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}+\sum_{i=1}^{2}(z_{i}^{2}D_{i}c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)})\nabla\Phi^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}+\sum_{i=1}^{2}(z_{i}^{2}D_{i}c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)})\nabla\Phi^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad=-(\partial_{t}+u^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}\cdot\nabla)\Delta\Phi^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},

and

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
d=2,3,

\lambda\leq c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}(0)
and
\bigl(c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}(0),c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}(0),c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}(0),u^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}(0),u^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}(0),u^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}(0)\bigr)
belong to
H^{5}
, then system (
4.5
)-(
4.6
) admits a unique solution
(c_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)},u^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)})
in
C([0,T_{0}];H^{5})\cap L^{2}(0,T_{0};H^{6})
.

\bullet
Analysis of boundary layer solutions.

For boundary layers, we only deal with the boundary layers near
y=0
, the same results hold for boundary layers near
y=1.

By comparing the leading order of Navier-Stokes equations, we have
\partial_{\xi}p_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(-2)}=0=p_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(-2)}(x^{\prime},\xi\rightarrow+\infty)
, which leads to
p_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(-2)}=0
. The next order
(c_{\scriptscriptstyle i,LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},\Phi_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},v_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},w_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},p_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(-1)})
satisfy the following system:

(4.7)

\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\partial^{2}_{\xi}c_{\scriptscriptstyle i,LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}+z_{i}\partial_{\xi}c_{\scriptscriptstyle i,LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}\partial_{\xi}\Phi_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}+z_{i}(\gamma_{i}(x^{\prime},0)+c_{\scriptscriptstyle i,LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)})\partial_{\xi}^{2}\Phi_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}=0,\quad 0<\xi,t<+\infty,\\
-\partial_{\xi}^{2}\Phi_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}=\rho_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},\\
\partial_{\xi}^{2}v_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}=0,\\
-\nu\partial_{\xi}^{2}w_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}+\partial_{\xi}p_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(-1)}=0,\\
\partial_{\xi}w_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}=0.\end{array}\right.

We implement the system (
4.7
) with the boundary conditions:

(c_{\scriptscriptstyle i,LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},v_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},w_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)})(x^{\prime},\xi=0)=0\quad\hbox{and}\quad(c_{\scriptscriptstyle i,LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},\Phi_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},\partial_{\xi}\Phi_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},v_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},w_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},p_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(-1)})(x^{\prime},\xi\rightarrow+\infty)=0.

Thus we can take
(c_{\scriptscriptstyle i,LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},\Phi_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},v_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},w_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},p_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(-1)})=0
, and by inserting them into the equations of
(c_{\scriptscriptstyle i,LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)},\Phi_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)},v_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)},w_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)},p_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)})
, we obtain

(4.8)

\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\partial^{2}_{\xi}c_{\scriptscriptstyle i,LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}+z_{i}\gamma_{i}(x^{\prime},0)\partial_{\xi}^{2}\Phi_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}=0,\quad 0<\xi,t<+\infty,\\
-\partial_{\xi}^{2}\Phi_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}=\rho_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)},\\
\partial_{\xi}^{2}v_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}=0,\\
-\nu\partial_{\xi}^{2}w_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}+\partial_{\xi}p_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}=0,\\
\partial_{\xi}w_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}=0.\end{array}\right.

We implement the system (
4.8
) with the boundary conditions:

(c_{\scriptscriptstyle i,LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)},v_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)},w_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)})(x^{\prime},\xi=0)=0\quad\hbox{and}\quad(c_{\scriptscriptstyle i,LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)},\Phi_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)},\partial_{\xi}\Phi_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)},v_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)},w_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)},p_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)})(x^{\prime},\xi\rightarrow+\infty)=0.

Thus we have
(c_{\scriptscriptstyle i,LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)},\Phi_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)},v_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)},w_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)},p_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)})=0
and by inserting them into the equations of

(c_{\scriptscriptstyle i,LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)},\Phi_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)},v_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)},w_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)},p_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)})
, we obtain

(4.9)

\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\partial^{2}_{\xi}c_{\scriptscriptstyle i,LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}+z_{i}\gamma_{i}(x^{\prime},0)\partial_{\xi}^{2}\Phi_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}=0,\quad 0<\xi,t<+\infty,\\
-\partial_{\xi}^{2}\Phi_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}=\rho_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)},\\
\partial_{\xi}^{2}v_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}=0,\\
-\nu\partial_{\xi}^{2}w_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}+\partial_{\xi}p_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}=0,\\
\partial_{\xi}w_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}=0.\end{array}\right.

To match the boundary condition
\rho^{\varepsilon}|_{\partial\Omega}=0
, we implement the system (
4.9
)
with the boundary conditions:

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{2}z_{i}c_{\scriptscriptstyle i,LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}(x,\xi=0)=\rho_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}(x^{\prime},\xi=0)=-\rho^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}(x^{\prime},y=0)=\Delta\Phi^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}(x^{\prime},y=0),

\displaystyle(v_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)},w_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)})(x^{\prime},\xi=0)=0\quad\hbox{and}\quad(c_{\scriptscriptstyle i,LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)},\Phi_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)},\partial_{\xi}\Phi_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)},v_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)},w_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)},p_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)})(x^{\prime},\xi\rightarrow+\infty)=0.

In view of
z_{1}\gamma_{1}(x^{\prime},0)+z_{2}\gamma_{2}(x^{\prime},0)=0
, one has

\partial^{2}_{\xi}(c_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}+c_{\scriptscriptstyle 2,LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)})=0,

which implies
c_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}=-c_{\scriptscriptstyle 2,LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}
and
\rho_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}=(z_{1}-z_{2})c_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}
. By inserting them and
-\partial_{\xi}^{2}\Phi_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}=\rho_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}
into the equation of
c_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}
, we achieve

\partial_{\xi}^{2}\rho_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}-z_{1}(z_{1}-z_{2})\gamma_{1}(x^{\prime},0)\rho_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}=0.

As a result, we deduce that

(4.10)

\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\rho_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}(x^{\prime},\xi,t)=\Delta\Phi^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}(x^{\prime},0,t)\exp{-\sqrt{z_{1}(z_{1}-z_{2})\gamma_{1}(x^{\prime},0)}\xi},\\
c_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}(x^{\prime},\xi,t)=\frac{1}{z_{1}-z_{2}}\Delta\Phi^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}(x^{\prime},0,t)\exp{-\sqrt{z_{1}(z_{1}-z_{2})\gamma_{1}(x^{\prime},0)}\xi},\\
c_{\scriptscriptstyle 2,LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}(x^{\prime},\xi,t)=\frac{-1}{z_{1}-z_{2}}\Delta\Phi^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}(x^{\prime},0,t)\exp{-\sqrt{z_{1}(z_{1}-z_{2})\gamma_{1}(x^{\prime},0)}\xi},\\
\Phi_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}(x^{\prime},\xi,t)=\frac{-\Delta\Phi^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}(x^{\prime},0,t)}{z_{1}(z_{1}-z_{2})\gamma_{1}(x^{\prime},0)}\exp{-\sqrt{z_{1}(z_{1}-z_{2})\gamma_{1}(x^{\prime},0)}\xi},\\
v_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}=w_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}=p_{\scriptscriptstyle LB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}=0.\end{array}\right.

For
(c_{\scriptscriptstyle i,RB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(k)},\Phi_{\scriptscriptstyle RB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(k)},v_{\scriptscriptstyle RB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(k)},w_{\scriptscriptstyle RB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(k)},p_{\scriptscriptstyle RB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(k)})
, similarly one has

(c_{\scriptscriptstyle i,RB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},\Phi_{\scriptscriptstyle RB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},v_{\scriptscriptstyle RB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},w_{\scriptscriptstyle RB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},p_{\scriptscriptstyle RB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(-1)})=0,

(c_{\scriptscriptstyle i,RB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)},\Phi_{\scriptscriptstyle RB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)},v_{\scriptscriptstyle RB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)},w_{\scriptscriptstyle RB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)},p_{\scriptscriptstyle RB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)})=0,

and

(4.11)

\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\rho_{\scriptscriptstyle RB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}(x^{\prime},\eta,t)=\Delta\Phi^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}(x^{\prime},1,t)\exp{-\sqrt{z_{1}(z_{1}-z_{2})\gamma_{1}(x^{\prime},1)}\eta},\\
c_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,RB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}(x^{\prime},\eta,t)=\frac{1}{z_{1}-z_{2}}\Delta\Phi^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}(x^{\prime},1,t)\exp{-\sqrt{z_{1}(z_{1}-z_{2})\gamma_{1}(x^{\prime},1)}\eta},\\
c_{\scriptscriptstyle 2,RB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}(x^{\prime},\eta,t)=\frac{-1}{z_{1}-z_{2}}\Delta\Phi^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}(x^{\prime},1,t)\exp{-\sqrt{z_{1}(z_{1}-z_{2})\gamma_{1}(x^{\prime},1)}\eta},\\
\Phi_{\scriptscriptstyle RB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}(x^{\prime},\eta,t)=\frac{-\Delta\Phi^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}(x^{\prime},1,t)}{z_{1}(z_{1}-z_{2})\gamma_{1}(x^{\prime},1)}\exp{-\sqrt{z_{1}(z_{1}-z_{2})\gamma_{1}(x^{\prime},1)}\eta},\\
v_{\scriptscriptstyle RB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}=w_{\scriptscriptstyle RB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}=p_{\scriptscriptstyle RB}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}=0.\end{array}\right.

\bullet
Analysis of initial layer solutions.

For initial layers, the leading order
(c_{\scriptscriptstyle i,I}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},u_{\scriptscriptstyle I}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},p_{\scriptscriptstyle I}^{\scriptscriptstyle(-2)})
satisfy the following system

(4.12)

\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\partial_{\tau}c_{\scriptscriptstyle i,I}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}=0,\quad x\in\Omega,\tau>0,\\
\rho_{\scriptscriptstyle I}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}=0,\\
\partial_{\tau}u_{\scriptscriptstyle I}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}+\nabla p_{\scriptscriptstyle I}^{\scriptscriptstyle(-2)}=0,\\
\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits u_{\scriptscriptstyle I}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}=0.\end{array}\right.

We implement the system (
4.12
) with the boundary conditions:

(c_{\scriptscriptstyle i,I}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},u_{\scriptscriptstyle I}^{\scriptscriptstyle(0)},p_{\scriptscriptstyle I}^{\scriptscriptstyle(-2)})(x^{\prime},y,\tau\rightarrow+\infty)=0.
